The boat sailed on under the moon, riding a little rougher tonight, rocking with us. In the early morning, sunlight slid through the window and brushed across my eyes, easing me up out of a dream. I cracked my eyes against the brightness and turned to Windsor beside me, her smooth, fragrant shoulder bare above the sheet, and I smiled, leaned down to kiss her forehead, then got up and dressed.

A new day, and Mory was already on breakfast, the other five out fishing on the deck. So, I grabbed a stool, sat off to the side, and started my own day on the resource fishing rod. The first pole was a lounge chair blueprint. White, a comfortable chair to enjoy your leisure on. Four wood and 10 iron nails, another quality of life trinket.

The resource rod generally coughed up nothing high tier, though it still beat the basic rod by a mile. And that first rod had given me almost nothing of value in six full days. I bought four wood, crafted 10 iron nails, and built the chair, then carried it to the bow and stretched out on it, and it rocked far comfier than the wooden stool.

Back to work. Huawa fluttered over and settled in my arms and I stroked her little head because she hadn't had purple crystal in two days and she couldn't grow off hunting in the sea either. So apparently combat did nothing for her. The gym mountain turtle had told me her kind grows by eating elemental crystals of their own attribute or special crystals like purple crystal in a pinch.

The problem was I had no way to get elemental crystals and the purple crystal route ran unstable and burning my talent to chase it wasn't worth it. The rod twitched and the day's second item came up. A lumberyard blueprint, white, 300 wood and 20 iron. The text scolding that a territory without logging is incomplete. A far better blueprint than the lounge chair despite sharing the same white tear.

Windsor wandered over, sat beside me, and pressed a cup of water into my hand. I asked why she hadn't slept a while longer, and she smiled gently and said she'd always woken at this hour. She was used to it, and I teased her that last night had been a little different. She shot me a charming glare and went back to ignoring the man who never stopped needling her.

After last night, though, the two of us had become inseparable. No distance left between us. Before, Windsor had always set herself a rung below me, a subordinate, and only now did she truly feel like an equal I could talk to as a partner. I was glad to see it because I'd have no end of subordinates down the road.

But I'd never find another lord to stand beside me. So, a hero companion who could meet me as an equal was the best I could hope for. They wouldn't betray me, and they put my interest first in everything, and simply perfect. I took her hand, smiling, and feeling the warmth in it, she went happy and easy, plainly content with the life she had now.

The last item came up. A black sphere. A poison elemental bomb. A special item that inflicts poison corrosion on creatures with power scaling against the targets poison resistance. And that lit me up. A special item and an offensive one. Another small trump card. And I tucked it away certain it would matter someday. And the resource rod's uses for the day were spent.

A solid hall overall. I ate breakfast. And while I was eating, Chammo dropped another round of announcements. The first congratulated everyone on clearing the first week and pushed us to keep working. The second opened the arena, telling us to read the rules ourselves and take part. The third warned that random events would start cropping up on the journey ahead, so be ready.

Three announcements, one piece of information each, and the first confirmed a week in this world runs 6 days. The second opened an arena plainly meant to push lords to sharpen their personal strength, while the third that random events would occur, was too vague to do much with, and random means uncertain, good or bad with no telling which.

Run into something good and great, but run into something bad, like that gym mountain turtle yesterday. And if it had been hostile, I'd have died a hundred times over. It had been kind though, and I'd walked away rich. The kind of change that makes you eager and uneasy at once. I opened my personal panel and found a new column, arena, sitting beside territory with three options inside.

Enter, points ranking, and points mall. The points mall caught me off guard since I hadn't expected to buy things here. I opened it and found no basic materials, just the relatively precious stuff, equipment, blueprints, commoner summon cards, and the like. Plenty of attempting. Then I opened the points ranking and saw it empty. No one on it yet.

And finally, I clicked enter and a wall of rules popped up. The arena randomly matches opponents. Winner gains points and loser sheds them. And spectators may bet, and the two combatants may also wager privately, and there is no death in the arena. Points can be exchanged for items in the points mall with the spent points deducted from your ranking total.

And the top 10,000 on the arena points ranking get to join the weekend two domains competition. The first three were just house rules, but the last two demanded real attention. 5 and lose my spot in the weekend two domains competition. Confronting the demon domain would be dangerous, no question. But win there and the rewards were huge.

Once I'd absorbed all that, I set down my bowl and chopsticks, went to lie on the bed, and chose to enter. The moment I crossed over, a flood of noisy information slammed my ears, and my mind went briefly foggy. I caught my footing fast and started taking stock, and I looked like myself from the real world, though I understood this was only a consciousness body.

I was standing on a corridor at the very top of the structure with rows of seats curving below into a full circle, a round arena at the dead center, and light screens hanging above the ring to let the crowd watch the matches clearly. Someone called out asking if even the boss had shown up and I turned to find Wong Mock.

He'd been talking with me every night lately and running errands for me, so I felt fairly warm toward him. I gave him a nod and said I'd just come to look around, then asked how he'd been. He grinned and said that thanks to my help, he was doing pretty well now, then offered to walk me through the arena since he'd already played around.

I let him lead me down the corridor to a staircase, and we went down and each picked a seat. A red panel popped up in front of both of us with three buttons: start, watch, and shop.

Wangmok pointed me through it because Stark matched you with an opponent for a bout and watch let you pick a match to play on your own panel though most people just watched the big screen at the ring center where the hottest fights aired. Shop sold things mostly food and drink all virtual but uncannily real to eat and all paid for an actual white stone which he figured few people would bother with.

Simple enough. And I asked if he'd fought a match yet and what it was like and he had one match and he said you drop into a random arena. His had been a grassland and he'd heard there were oceans, snow mountains, and other environments, too. He laid out his whole experience in the secondhand details he'd gathered, and I said I'd play around and hit start.

The next instant, I stood in a desert facing a burly, middle-aged man with a great axe and an iron shield. I checked him first. No AU, level five. The rest hidden, which didn't surprise me since Wang Mock had told me arena matches hide all opponent attributes, even quality. While I read him, he read me back. Hashin, level four.

My level still suppressed on purpose, though I figured one more sea hunt today would carry me to five. No saw a name matching the famous boss and didn't think twice about it since he'd never seen what I looked like and the world was full of men named Hashin. What he did notice was my level, one below his, and he laughed certain of easy points.

He called me unlucky, said that while the arena spared your life, the pain was real, and urged me to just surrender and save myself the hurt. I didn't care in the slightest because everyone had been cooped up on their ships for ages and the first time they met fellow blue star folk, the urge to show off ran hot.

I told him plainly that I wouldn't surrender and he could start whenever he liked. He shook his head and said I wouldn't shed tears until I saw the coffin, then charged. I didn't move an inch, only raised my staff and spoke the freezing spell, and a threat of white light gathered and converged on his legs. His steps went heavy, and when he looked down, he found ice crystals climbing up them.

He stared at me, stunned, blurting out that I had a talent, that my luck was too good since talents were still rare and he hadn't expected to draw one. He knew right then he'd lost, so he called out for me to stop. He surrendered, and I cut off the water arrow I'd been about to lose. Then he asked, hesitant, whether I might happen to be the real Hashin, the boss himself.

But I ignored him and simply left because chatting with every single person would bleed my whole day dry. Watching me walk off without a word, his hunch only hardened, and he kicked himself for not asking to add me as a friend. A good chance missed. Back in my seat, I checked the ranking and saw I'd picked up 10 points.

Wang Moch was gone, often a match somewhere, so I started another without waiting. This time, I drew a thoroughly ordinary opponent and finished him with one freezing spell and one water arrow, and the points rose another 10. So, a win was worth 10 flat. And I just didn't know how the scoring would shift at higher levels.

After that, I opened the shop, spent one white stone on a juice, cheaper than outside since it was virtual, and took a sip, and the taste came through identical to the real thing. Wang Mach reappeared right then. His match finished and the first thing out of him was a curse that he'd run into bake.

He asked if I was done too and I said his face told me he'd lost and to tell me exactly what happened. I knew big seal well enough since she sat second only to me. So I paid close attention to anything about her and Wang Mock said she definitely had a talent. Certainly something tied to perception.

Their venue had been a forest and the moment he appeared he'd gone invisible with his own talent. But she found him before long anyway and he'd kept shifting position and it had done nothing. That surprised me that he had a talent of his own now, invisibility, which was life-saving and let him land sneak attacks. Supportive but genuinely good.

As for him calling Bake Seiel's talent perception type, I wasn't ready to commit. All I could say was that her talent gave her very sharp perception. Not that the talent itself was a perception talent. I asked what level she was because if her level ran high and her talent quality wasn't low, I might actually lose to her.

He said it was level five. Not too worrying because I was nearly level five myself and as long as her talent wasn't legendary quality, I had no fear of her and I traded a few more words with him, then left the arena. Outside, Windsor and the others were already fishing and Mory was on lunch and I hadn't realized how long I'd spent in the arena since it had felt like nothing.

After lunch, I started the afternoon hunt, smooth and uneventful, 2 hours gone fast. When the boat surfaced, I sent everyone back to fishing and went to check my assets. Just over 4,200 white stone now. Still more than 800 short of the upgrade materials. I'd have to wait for evening when the afternoon's catch plus the money tree hall should cover it.

I'd also hit level 5 on this dive. So I pulled up my information. Level five, strength 27, agility 27, defense 26, wisdom 25, mana 250. A hero, ice element messenger with spirit eye, green water arrow technique, purple freezing spell, a green staff ring, and sea star armor, and 100 transformations at epic no title. My base stats were nearly 30 thanks mostly to the turtle spirit fruits.

And as long as I didn't cross a legendary level being, I was basically unbeatable at my own level. In this world, the gulf between qualities was simply a chasm and shorter than possibly heaven-defying luck, eating treasure after treasure without repeats. It couldn't be bridged, and since I couldn't upgrade the boat yet, I went back into the arena.

I played all afternoon and never once hit a decent opponent, just a string of easy wins. And when I came out, it was evening with Mory's dinner waiting. I sold the afternoon's catch first, keeping only a white quality ore blueprint, then ate. After dinner, I shook the money tree for 598, which put my assets over 5,300. So, I bought the materials and hit upgrade.

When it finished, I checked the result. Wooden boat, level 6, 40 m x 12. Facilities now ran to a core cabin, two large rooms, four small rooms, a warehouse, a public restroom, the fishing gallery, a mast and a helm.

Power came from multior linkage power propulsion with its technologies stacked as ship drainage at level three, ship thermal energy at level one, ship automation at level one, multiore linkage propulsion at level two, and ship diving weakened at level two. Special ability diving. The jump was real this time. A mast and a helm added one more large room, two more small rooms, and the restroom split off into a separate public one.

Though the core cabin kept its own, and the trend was clear. Down the road, I'd be fully separated from the commoners, which didn't surprise me because a lord can't be rubbing shoulders with commoners all day. I only had to manage the heroes well, issue orders, and let them carry them out. Then I looked ahead to the next upgrade since level six was a threshold.

2,000 wood, 4,000 iron nails, 200 copper block, and one ship core. Beyond the heavier basic materials, a ship core was now required. And I didn't know whether it'd be needed for every upgrade from here or only this jump from six to seven. Either way, I'd have to find a way to stockpile ship cores just in case.

I didn't even know if they could be bought since probably no one yet understood what a ship core actually did, but I could test the market tomorrow. The basic materials came to 10,000 white stone, about 5 days of saving at my current rate. Fine, no rush. And when the money came, I'd upgrade the power technology first.

I closed the panel, settled Huawa and little blue as usual, undressed and got into bed, and Windsor's smooth body folded against mine, and I held her and kissed her cheek. Then came the day's use of hundred transformations. And first, the day's paddle blueprint. Not worth much, so I just used it, and it became a green commoner summon card, which I pocketed for tomorrow.

Next, I took out the leftover green ore blueprint and ran 100 transformations, getting a navigator summon card, blue, that summons a navigator to steer you clear of countless disasters at sea. This one wasn't random. It specified the profession and it landed perfectly since my new boat had a helm and a mast.

I'd summon him tomorrow and put him on the wheel for good so little blue wouldn't have to stand guard every day. For the last use, I burned a white commoner summon card I'd picked off Wang Mock's trade list. Ran 100 transformations and looked at the result with a flat little sigh. Another summon card just bumped to green.

Three uses today. All three summon cards. Nothing to write home about beyond the small windfall of the navigator. Then I caught myself and almost laughed at how spoiled I'd gotten, complaining about green quality items. The last few days of fat halls had gone to my head until I'd forgotten this was the normal and only my normal at that since most players never came close to my floor.

With the day's tasks done, I skipped the chat since it would only eat into what mattered. I flipped over, pinned Windsor beneath me, and she let out a small gasp that I caught with a kiss, leaving her only muffled sounds. Bit by bit, the whole room held nothing but heavy breathing and soft murmurss.

The next day, I opened my eyes to find the blanket gone, both of us lying bare on the bed, and the sight of Windsor's pale skin and fine figure landed like a blow, blood going straight to both ends. She opened her eyes to find me already coming toward her, and she went from groggy to awake to dazed, then answered, then finally went limp.

After the storm passed, I held her and kissed her cheek until we both come back down. She murmured that we ought to get up. It was late, leaning into me with a faint flush still on her face. I agreed and started dressing, saying as I did that it felt much hotter today than yesterday, that this week's disaster was likely extreme heat.

I'd felt the temperature shift sharply, and for the first time the blanket had ended up kick clean off in the night, which had never happened before. I needed to get ahead of this early. Windsor agreed it was hotter, but said it was still bearable, dressing slower than me since she had more layers.

I said I should still plan ahead, and she fixed me with a steady look and told me she believed I could handle these things because she'd watched me climb from a single wooden plank to all this. I smiled and told her not to worry. Walking out, it was hotter outside than in. The sun gone white and blasting harsh light down.

I used yesterday's three summon cards first and three men appeared. One middle-aged and two younger. And I checked them over. Lee Rrook, excellent quality, level five, strength 27, agility 25, defense 26, a navigator who could judge wind direction, draw sea charts, and pilot ships. Wang Yui and Wang Rim, both excellent, both level three. Both sailors who could paddle, manage the mast, and maintain the ship.

I hadn't expected the two random cards to both produce professions and sailors at that, a role I could use right now. All three bowed and greeted me as their lord, and I waved off the formality and told Leuk I'd leave the upcoming voyage in his hands.

I took the map fragment out and passed it to him, telling him the black dot on it was our destination, and he glanced it over and assured me he'd keep the ship sailing safely. I sent the three of them off to their work, then checked the survival point ranking.

I closed the ranking and looked at my white stone. Just over 200 left. Nothing I could do with that. I went to the lounge chair at the bow, stretched out, and started working the resource rod while turning over what I needed to do. And the top priority was surviving this week's extreme heat. I opened the technology section to hunt for anything useful.

And there were relevant entries: cabin cooling, ship cooling, ship temperature control, but not one met its activation conditions. Plenty of others tempted me, too. Ship flying tech, and the enviable weapon systems among them, and none could be activated yet. Since technology was no help right now, I'd have to find another road. Right then, the first rod item came up and my face lit because it glowed blue. A commoner summon card, blue, the kind that lets highquality commoners push a territory above its neighbors and make new knowledge easier to learn. No chance I'd waste the summoning a commoner.

It had to feed 100 transformations, a purple at minimum and a gold if luck smiled. Soon after, another commoner summon card, this one white, and I burned it outright. One last pull left on the resource rod, and I quietly hoped for a surprise.

I didn't have to wait long because the last item came up glowing, only white, and I deflated a little reading it. That caught my attention because I'd seen this thermostat before.

I opened my personal shop and there it was. This week's special offer at 500 white stone, which I'd noticed earlier and skipped. So, the key to surviving this disaster was the thermostat. Except it only held a temperature. It couldn't change the one you had. Then, I noticed the shop had a new item today. Ice cubes. 10 white stone each.

I bought one and it came as a cube roughly half a meter to a side, which clicked the whole picture into place. Shanmo's plan was for everyone to drop the temperature by buying ice and then trap it with a thermostat, riding out the scorching weak that way. Only there was a catch, because the thermostat merely slowed the loss, so the ice still melted, which meant buying cubes endlessly.

And one cube couldn't cool a space, so holding a livable temperature would bleed a fortune in white stone, a dull knife sewing through your savings. Once I understood the trap, I smiled because I knew freezing spell. Why couldn't I just sell ice myself? Price it one or two whitest stone under the shop and there'd be no shortage of buyers because the sea is the last place that ever run short of water.

So, this was a business with zero cost to me. The thought was so good I actually laughed out loud and it seemed I might not need 5 or 6 days to upgrade the boat after all. Windsor called me right then. Breakfast was ready and I ate fast. I told them we wouldn't fish today.

We'd go straight to hunting because I didn't have enough white stone for even a thermostat and I needed to make some money first. Nobody objected. By the time the boat surfaced, I'd stopped.

Comparable to the hunting hall itself. 10 hours a day would be 4,500 white stone. And the moment that number landed, my head went hot because I could upgrade the boat again in 2 days.

I sent the others off to fish and took the items Windsor handed me from the hunt. Sold the basic material straight off, leaving three pieces. And at that point, the polite thing is to let the camera drift away. Heat woke me, not the alarm. And even outdoors, within a small bubble, it worked like a little air conditioner and made the heat survivable again. The trouble was that the warehouse itself couldn't go without cooling or the supplies would rot. So, I pulled a crisis warning device. No, a thermostat blueprint, white quality, a simple machine for holding a steady temperature with a flavor line daring me to figure out its uses.

It wanted 20 iron, 10 copper, and five electronic components. So, I bought the materials and built it. Set a couple of hands to shifting ice cubes into the warehouse and got the unit running until the room held a stable chill. With that sorted, I finally pulled out last night's gold hero card.

I was honestly buzzing about what would step out of it, so I used it in a flash of golden light folded down to reveal her. She was tall and lean in a black bodysuit that hid nothing with cropped black hair and faintly red pupils. And the detail that stopped me cold was the pair of cat ears on her head and the black tail flicking at the base of her spine.

A beasteared girl of all the roles I could have gotten. She bowed slightly, laid the hand holding a short black blade across her chest, and greeted me as her lord, her voice flat, her face cold, every inch the aloof type. When I asked her name, she told me to call her Dark Moon and spirit eye laid out the rest.

She was a legendary, only level three, a night hunter, which meant a warrior, and the short blade in her grip confirmed it. Her stats ran 30ome across strength, agility, defense, and wisdom with 350 mana, skills called darkness blade, darkness erosion, and moon shadow. A green shadow blade in hand. Loyalty pinned at 100.

Her affinity sat at 55, the friendly tier, which actually surprised me since that was five points above where Windsor had started, and it made me wonder whether the cold front was just a mask. A warrior also rounded out my roster nicely because I wanted my heroes to cover different ground rather than stepping on each other's toes.

Then I remembered the dark element skill stone gathering dust in my space, so I dug it out. It was shadow stealth blue quality letting the user melted to shadow to hide body and aura with a 30% damage bonus on strikes launched from the dark and the only requirement was being a dark element messenger.

I handed it over and told her she qualified perfectly and she glanced at the stone, caught my nod and used it on the spot. A chime confirmed her affinity toward me had climbed another five which I'd expected. I followed it up by pulling out a green Ling Yen goldf fruit and three other heavenly treasures.

A spirit yawn purple grape, a spirit yawn blue luo, and a profound yan gins singing, one of each, and offered them a cross. Dark Moon's composure cracked for a second. Surprised I'd be carrying four separate attribute fruits with none lower than green. But instead of taking them, she just stood there confused.

I asked what was wrong, and she finally said, looking up into my eyes, that she couldn't accept rewards she hadn't earned, that she'd done nothing yet. That made me smile because I hadn't pegged her for such a stubborn sense of honor. So, I asked why she'd taken the skill stone. Then and she answered that she'd assumed that one was a welcome gift, which left me without a comeback.

I told her to take these two and get strong fast, that calling herself unworthy was nonsense when she was already mine, and that from now on, her job was guarding me personally. Her loyalty was a hundred, so I didn't worry for a heartbeat about her motives. Something flickered behind her eyes, her lashes trembling, and without another word, she took the fruits and ate them one by one.

The four treasures raised her stats by 19 points total. Five each into strength, agility, and defense, and four into wisdom, and her affinity jumped another 12 to 72, the intimate tier. That's the gift of a strong foundation, I marveled. Since she'd hit intimate in a single afternoon when Windsor had taken so much longer.

I told her to follow me out, and as I left the warehouse, she startled me by saking straight into my shadow, which is not how I'd pictured shadow stealth working at all. Out on deck, I had her surface and introduced her to the others. Windsor offered a gentle smile in her name. Dark Moon answered with just her own, and I told Windsor to walk her through our setup.

The two of them headed back to the core cabin together, and I picked up the resource fishing rod to start the day's draws. The first thing I reeled in was a green item quality enhancement card, which boosted an item to a higher tier when it worked. I pulled out the glass bottle of Azure Sea Dragon blood and used the card on it, and nothing changed.

The blood still reading blue when I checked. The enhancement had failed, and while that stung, a green card carries lousy odds. So, I braced for it. The next two draws gave me a green commoner summon card and another white thermostat blueprint, which surprised me since apparently these weren't one per customer.

I jumped on the trade channel and spent around 400 white stones scooping up 50 electronic components while they were still cheap because once people started mass building thermostats, that price would only climb. After breakfast, I started the day's hunt and it went the way these things usually did, smooth and lucrative, netting me over 2,300 white stone.

On top of that, I picked up a white militia soldier nest and a green skill stone spirit. I named the stone shield green quality, raising my defense by 10% whenever I cast shield. And since I'd been running around with no defensive skill at all, I learned it myself immediately. Then I went to handle the flower producer.

It was sitting at level 26 kg of flour a day, which no longer stretched far enough. The jump to level three wanted 80 wood, 20 iron block, 20 copper block, and 400 white stone. So, I bought what I lacked and pushed it through, doubling output to 12 kg, roughly 24 lb.

Between me, Windsor, Dark Moon, and 10 commoners, I had 13 mouths, so it was tight, but workable, especially since fishing still topped us up, even if a steady diet of bread gets dull enough to make a man weep. I had 1,800 odd white stone left after that.

So, I spent 300 more on iron and copper and built the thermostat I'd drawn that morning, planting it in my own core cabin since my room had no cooling at all. A few commoners fed ice cubes in until the air went pleasantly cool. I dropped onto the sofa, Huawa lounging across the top of my head while Windsor and Dark Moon murmured to each other on the bed.

I plucked Huawa down, tucked her into the crook of my arm, and fished out the purple crystal, and her eyes flared with the light before she clutched it and started eating. While she nawed away, I worked through my plans. Cooling was still half-solved since I'd handled my own room, but not my peoples. The men shared one large room and the women a small one.

So I needed at least two more thermostats to make their quarters livable. Somebody out there had surely pulled spare blueprints by now. So I opened trade and posted three buy orders, 100 whit stone each, for thermostat blueprints, planning to put two in the living quarters and stash a third in my one empty small room for storing ice.

I took stock of my real estate while I was at it. Two large rooms, one for the men, one packed with ice, and four small ones. Of the small rooms, the women had one, the kitchen claimed another. The seawater converter and flower producer filled a third and the last set empty, earmarked for more ice. By my math, I could start selling ice cubes as soon as tomorrow.

Everyone would likely have a thermostat by then, and under tomorrow's heat, a player without cooling simply wouldn't make it. The coal always started on the fourth day of the week, like clockwork. I carried Huawa back outside to keep producing ice, doing it the same way as before, conjuring cubes myself while two hands hauled them off, resting whenever my mana bottomed out.

I'd barely settled in when a male commoner came over with a blueprint in his hands. I took it, waved him back, and read it. It was a sun shade blueprint, white quality, a parasol to fend off the summer sun. Wanting three wood and five cloth, and since something like that was exactly what the deck needed, I built it at once.

Opened up, the sun shade was big enough to cover two lounge chairs. So, I stretched out under it and resumed making ice in a steady rhythm. When my mana ran dry, I switched over to the arena because those points mattered and I had every intention of competing hard in the coming demon domain event. The prize that drew me was ship cores.

I tried buying them on the trade channel, but nobody was fool enough to part with one. So, I'd come up empty. I understood their weight now. Without ship cores, a ship couldn't be upgraded, and that meant ship cores alone decided how far I could expand across the sea, which made me determined to haul home as many as I could grab.

The day passed without incident, and evening rolled in. Once everyone had eaten and scattered to their rooms, I tallied the afternoon's work. I built three more thermostats from the freshly bought blueprints and set them in the living quarters as planned, and I bought up a heap of cotton wool and cloth, run off a batch of bedding with the bedding set blueprint, and left Mory to outfit the beds.

Back in my own room, I found Windsor and Dark Moon sitting on the sofa, and it hit me that I'd never sorted out where Dark Moon was supposed to sleep. I obviously couldn't park her in my room and wreck my privacy with Windsor, so I frowned, turning the puzzle over.

Then Windsor, seeing me walk in with the cooler, beat me to it, suggesting she and Dark Moon take the bed tonight while I made do with the sofa. That stopped me for a second. Back on Blueest Star, I'd known plenty of married men who got exiled to the couch, some of whom even splurged on a nice one for the occasion, and I hadn't expected to join their ranks out here.

Reading my hesitation as refusal, Windsor crossed over, lifted the cooler from my hand, hooked her arm through mine, and tugged me down onto the sofa. She pointed out that Dark Moon had only just arrived and I couldn't very well stick her in with the crew, so I should just put up with it a while.

She kept shaking my arm and playing the spoiled card, and I looked from her to Dark Moon, who' turned her face away from me entirely, and wondered how those two had gotten so close in one day. I told them it was fine. The sofa was plenty big, and I'd take it.

Honestly, I had no objection since rooming the two girls together would only help them bond, and going without Windsor for a stretch was hardly a tragedy. I had arena points to grind anyway, and that wasn't about chasing one night's fun.

Windsor thanked me with a kiss on the cheek and led Dark Moon to bed, where Windsor undressed, but Dark Moon kept her clothes on, which didn't surprise me given how new we still were. I stripped down without ceremony, ignoring Dark Moon's faint blush entirely, then collected the day's money tree hall, sprawled on the sofa, and opened Wang Mock's trade list.

I bought two more thermostat blueprints off it to push my insulation further, then laid out the rest of the day's poles, a green commoner summon card, a white soldier nest, and another white commoner summon card. The green one went first into 100 transformations. And what came back made me laugh out loud. It was a hero transfer card, special grade, granting its target a hero profession.

And the description spelled out what that meant. Infinite possibility, the chance to climb to higher tiers without quality holding you back. So that was the answer to a question that had nagged me. I'd never understood why the hero profession didn't just hand out raw combat power. And now I saw the truth. It boosted growth potential instead, which is worth far more than a flat strength bump.

I tucked it away for Huawa since she was already gold quality and this would make her a true legendary hero, assuming pets could even use it. Then I dropped the white soldier nest into 100 transformations to see if it would surprise me, and it did.

The white nest leapt two whole tiers to blue and came out as a rock giant soldier nest, letting me recruit rock giants, which just from the name sounded a cut above militia. My last slot went to a purple stone. And this time, I pulled two purple crystals at once. A small critical hit. Both bound for Huaha tomorrow.

With that done, I entered the arena and reappeared in the corridor, which was packed tonight since evening was when board players flooded in to kill time, farm competition points, and sharpen their footwork in real fights. I didn't doawle. I bought a virtual mask and slid into an empty seat. The mask being a necessity now that enough people recognize me on site.

And without it, I'd be sworn by chatter I had no patience for. Instead of jumping into a match, I scanned the active fights for anyone highranked, since watching better players broadened my read on the strange talents floating around out here, so I'd never freeze if I met something similar. I grabbed a drink while I browsed, one white stone being pocket changed to me now.

A small fair hand patted my shoulder and broke my concentration, and a petite girl with a startlingly full chest dropped into the seat beside me, a golden mask over her face. I gave her a glance. She wore a mini skirt and a short-sleeved white tank top with a blue cat printed on it, cheeks puffed out in a sulk.

She told me to quit hiding, that she'd already made me. And when I admitted I had no idea who she was, she introduced herself as Lee Kio and stuck out a small hand, saying it felt like fate to run into a big shot like me. Of course, I knew the name, I asked how she'd recognized me when I'd hidden all my information behind that mask.

And she said breezily that her eyes worked differently from other people's, her talent, and left it there. I wouldn't dream of pressing for the specifics since prying into a player's talent is about the rudest thing you can do. I asked why her arena ranking had slipped this season, whether she'd given up the boss seat or whether maybe Haguina was right that I simply had no real strength.

My expression went cold at that because what was that supposed to mean? And I asked whether the little girl knew how to hold a conversation at all. Calling her a little girl was a mistake. Her eyes went wide since being treated as a child was clearly her sorest spot.

And she snapped at me to open my eyes and look that she was 25, probably older than me, even puffing out her chest for emphasis, which made her point in more ways than one. I was honestly thrown both by her ferocity and by the age because the stereotype said foreign women ran tall and strong, and here was the opposite.

I apologized, told her it had been a slip of the tongue, and since Leia was in fact not small in the relevant sense, I didn't fight the point. Mllified, she crisply demanded I add her as a friend so we could trade good things down the line, complaining that she'd sent me requests over and over and I'd never once answered.

That was true, but only because I drowned in friend requests and just wiped them all at once. So, I'd never single her out. Though, now that she'd hunted me down in person, I wasn't about to refuse. I added her and she beamed and said she'd finally collected everyone she found interesting, then excused herself to go compete, patting my arm before vanishing from her seat into a match.

I shook my head and went back to scanning. And to my surprise, I found a fight worth watching. Hagawa against some opponent. My grudge with him couldn't be smoothed over. And frankly, I'd never grasped why he clung to me so doggedly. But none of that mattered now. What mattered was that I decided to deal with him. Because if I let his needling stand, every other player would learn to do the same.

And that I couldn't have. Kill the chicken to scare the monkeys. Since I meant to fight him eventually, I wasn't about to be careless. So, I pulled up his match to study him. Knowing yourself and your enemy being the shest road to never losing. The arena was hilly terrain, dotted with low mounds, and Hagawa Hina faced a burly man across it.

He wore black pants and a white long-sleeved shirt with a little braid in his hair, a dagger gripped in each hand, while the big man carried a steel saber and a full set of armor, obviously a tank warrior. When the match opened, Hagawa charged straight in, and the tank, plainly wary of his reputation, refused to meet it head-on, planting himself and waiting in a ready stance.

Hagawa closed fast and lashed out with both daggers, one at the head, one at the gut, but the tank simply tilted away from the high strike, ignored the low one entirely, and swung his saber through where Hagawahina stood. Hagawahina hadn't expected such reckless aggression. A man trading hits without flinching, and his face shifted as he bent low and rolled aside.

The tank pounced on the opening, striding in and chopping down again before Hagahina could find his feet, pressing the advantage hard, and Hagahena had no choice but to keep dodging. Backpedaling step after step, anger crept into his face, since one careless opening had handed the tank control of the whole fight. He'd wanted to keep his real strength hidden, but that was off the table now, unless he was willing to lose.

And Hagawa would rather die than lose to a nobody and become the channel's punchline. The tank, sensing the wind, bore in with the saber flashing, even releasing arcs of saber light. So, he was a professional, too. But this time, Hagawa stayed calm, a mocking little smile surfacing as the light came down. And then, he simply wasn't there.

The saber light struck bare ground and threw up dust. And when it cleared there were only a few gouged trenches where he'd been. The tank flinched and went vigilant and a figure resolved behind him. Hagawahina stabbing for his head and his lower spine at once. The tank reacted fast, rolling forward to escape. But before he could rise, Hagawahina vanished again and reappeared in front of him.

Too quick to answer and the dagger pierced his skull and ended it. The match closed. Even in victory, Hagawahina didn't look pleased because he just shown a slice of his real strength. And now every future foe would be on guard, which is exactly how it played out. Watching that fight, I felt I'd come away with something. That final trick of his was an earth element skill.

Something like earth evasion. The first time he'd blinked across the field, I tensed, half convinced he'd mastered space element magic, which would have made him a genuinely nasty problem. The kind of enemy you lose sleep over. But on a closer look, I'd caught the surface soil rippling each time he moved. And that told me the real story.

Earth element, not space. That came as a relief since earth skills are badly handicapped on the open sea. It also clarified why my own ice element messenger profession rated as purple tier because ice carries so many advantages out here where other professions wither. My weariness toward Hagawa ticked up, but I wasn't losing any sleep.

After that, I entered the arena proper and ran my own matches and I won every one of them. Not a single loss. 4 hours later, near midnight, I logged out. Looking at the two girls asleep on the bed, Windsor especially, draped in a silk pajamas I'd sewn for her in odd moments, I felt the heat rise in me again, because those pajamas lent her a mature allure that was frankly unfair.

I sighed, shut my eyes, and told myself out of sight, out of mind, and that sleeping was the wiser plan. Then something soft settled against my leg, and I opened my eyes to find Windsor perched there, cheeks flushed, very much awake. She'd heard me sigh and couldn't bear it. So, she crept over to keep me company, and I sat up more than a little pleased, and pulled her into my arms.

I pressed my forehead to hers and asked softly why she'd come since I thought she was asleep. She said she'd heard me sigh and wanted to be near me, trembling a little as my breath brushed her skin.

I smiled and asked whether keeping me company was all she had in mind, my hands sliding down to her waist and squeezing, and she went stiff and warned me to stop, that I'd wake Dark Moon and she'd die of mortification if she saw us.

I murmured that it was fine, that I'd be gentle and Dark Moon would never notice and shifted just enough that Windsor couldn't pretend she didn't feel how much I wanted her. She glanced at Dark Moon lying turned away from us, then lowered her head, shy and I understood her perfectly. So my hands kept moving and a quiet, careful little battle began under the covers.

Dark Moon lay with her back to us, face burning frozen, not daring to move a muscle, holding her pretend sleep together by sheer will. She'd felt Windsor slip out of bed earlier and simply hadn't known how to react, so she'd chosen to know nothing and let it run its course.

She hadn't taken Windsor for someone capable of this, not after the gentle, modest girl she'd talked with all day, and her mind churned as the soft, smothered sounds went on behind her. Then a sudden, shrill beeping tore through the cabin, and all three of us jolted, hearts lurching, Windsor and I, most of all, badly startled, and Windsor let out a small involuntary cry.

I held her close but had no time to soo her because I knew that sound the alarm. I scrambled up, yanked on my underwear, and bolted out of the cabin. Windsor understood at once, still shaky, so she cast a quick healing on herself to study her spirit and her strength, then pulled on the dress Dark Moon wordlessly handed her, slipping it on without meeting her eyes.

She was thoroughly mortified now, wondering how she'd ever face Dark Moon again and laying all the blame squarely on me. To her relief, Dark Moon barely reacted and simply walked out of the cabin and Windsor breeds easier and hurried after her. Outside, they found me already in it. Huawa, Little Blue, and a cluster of bowarmed commoners trading blows with a school of fish.

Windsor rushed to my side. I told her the levels were mostly low, and the only real problem was the sheer numbers, ordering her to clear out the higher tier sea monsters while Dark Moon shielded the commoners and held casualties to zero. Spirit eye had already swept the swarm for me. Most ran level three or four, but some hit level seven or eight.

They were nearly all flying swordfish. White quality. Their one trick, a flying sword that wasn't a water arrow, but a bolt of raw energy. To me, they posed no danger so long as I stayed sharp. But to my white quality commoners, they were lethal. Windsor and Dark Moon moved the moment I gave the word.

Windsor loosed light arrows, hunting the higher level monsters and slipping a healing onto wounded commoners between shots, while Huawa rained down small fireballs and threw the occasional fire shield or firebinding to cover anyone caught out. Dark Moon prowled the deck, and whenever a monster pushed in close, her dagger went black and dropped it in a single stroke.

So fast and so steady, she looked less like a fighter than a dancer, killing turned into a kind of grim, graceful performance. Little Blue stuck to its bread and butter, charging and biting. I shielded myself, then sent water arrows into the swarm, lobbing a freezing spell wherever the fish bunched up.

For me, these low-quality monsters were basically free money, though no experience since most ran below level five, and I only earned growth off kills near my own level. Quores and dropped items piled up on the sea around the boat, blood seeping out across the water. Then little blue gave an uneasy whimper, and I went rigid because I knew that crying meant it had sensed something far worse.

Two white flying swords tore up out of the sea, one chasing the other, both streaking for Windsor. She threw up a light shield, but it ate the first sword and the second was already on her, so Huawa and I both slammed shields into place ahead of her in the same breath. The two shields shattered and bled the swords force to nothing, and Windsor snapped up a fresh light shield.

I followed the trajectory back to its source. Two silver white sea monsters, 5 m long, floated on the surface, and one of them breached, showing me a flat blade-shaped body, furious that their ambush had failed to kill Windsor. The pair let out angry cries, and I caught myself genuinely startled that fish could make sound at all.

At their call, the lesser flying swordfish hammering the boat redoubled their assault, going frenzied, I checked the two big ones with spirit eye. Flying swordfish, blue quality, level 12, stats in the mid60s across strength, agility, and defense, and only two skills, flying sword and leap. I was almost insulted on their behalf since a blue monster with two skills, one of them a useless hop, barely earns the name.

Still, it was good news for me because more skills would have meant a real chance of losing people. Even so, only Windsor and Huawa could meaningfully touch these two, and Huawa could only stall. I made the call fast, telling Huawa and me to pin one while Windsor put down the other because there was no cleaner option.

Dark Moon was level three and her close-range kit was nearly worthless against ranged monsters skipping across the waves. So, I left her on crowd control. Walked one in firebinding and I layered a freezing spell on top while Windsor threw everything at her target. She opened with an entangling spell, but the fish slipped it with a leap.

So, as it hung in the air, she landed a bewilder that sent its midleap flying sword wide. The instant it splashed down, she cast entangling spell again. And this time, dazed by the bewilder, it failed to dodge and was bound. Windsor seized the moment, drilling a light arrow into it that punched a bloody hole without killing. Then stacking another bewilder to drag its movement slow.

The fish, goatated by pain, fired a flying sword that concentrated fully on her shield and broke it again. But she didn't flinch, just raised a new light shield and kept loosing arrows at its head, though its thrashing kept her shots off the kill. The blood kept welling though, and she could see she'd bleed it out if she stayed patient.

Even so, she fredded. Watching me because she knew my own strength made the other monster a brutal matchup. And she was exactly right. Huawa and I, two against one, couldn't land real damage and could only buy time.

The thing broke our control and surged toward the hole at speed, then flung a flying sword at Huawa rather than me, clearly reading her as the bigger threat, and only her small size and flight let her squeak past it. That was no kind of solution. If it reached the ship, it would gut the hole, whatever else happened.

Little Blue lunged to body block it, but the fish just leaped clean over Little Blue's bulk. And the moment it hit water, Huawa and I clamped control on it again. Both of us off attacks entirely now since our damage couldn't crack its defense. It tore free fast and bore in once more. I glanced at the deck and saw commoners already bleeding.

And I knew that if this dragged on the dying would start. Gritting my teeth, I dug through my space and pulled out an item. It was a poison element bomb, special grade, which eroded a creature with toxin. Its strength scaling against the target's poison resistance, and I just prayed it would melt the thing outright.

I held it ready, watching the flying swordfish close, planning to fling it the instant the monster came near enough to poison. I didn't dare blink. Then, Dark Moon was at my side, her clear, cold voice telling me to let it come closer, that she could handle it. I turned to her, baffled since I couldn't imagine what a level three close-range fighter could do to a blue monster.

But her loyalty was 100, and she'd never steer me wrong. So, if she said she had a way, she had a way. I asked anyway just to be sure and she answered calm that I could rest easy. She was confident reading the certainty in her eyes. I stopped hesitating and put the bomb away. Then cast her shadow lurk skill melting into my own shadow under the moonlight.

Huawa and I kept throwing control to keep stalling the monster because if I dropped my guard entirely, the thing might smell the trap and hang back. Soon it reached the boat, lashing its tail to throw up a wave, then breached and lunged straight at me, having clearly clocked my special status, and decided to take the lord out first.

Its huge mouth gaped, rows of teeth bared, eyes shot through with red, and just as it closed to bite, a black light flared across its vision. Pain bloomed through its body, and then it knew nothing.

I watched, stunned, as Dark Moon dropped out of the air to the deck, having surged up out of my shadow with her dagger gone black and a blade of darkness a full meter long streaming from its edge. One slash had taken the flying swordfish's head clean off its body, dead in a single blow.

I stared at her, struggling to process it because nothing about a level three hero gutting a blue monster like that made sense. She didn't notice my disbelief, just walked over and held out a skill stone she'd recovered, and I took it. It was a flying sword technique stone, blue quality, granting a 30% boost when releasing a flying sword with no exclusive class requirement, which meant Dark Moon could use it, too.

She was strong, but her one glaring weakness was the total absence of ranged attacks, a real liability in a duel, so I handed it straight back and told her it was hers. I'd considered running it through 100 transformations, but skill stones are precious and the talents too unpredictable to gamble one on. So, an immediate strength bump was the smarter play since a strong enough crew never wants for loot.

After a day of getting my measure, she didn't argue this time and simply used it. And a chime told me her affinity had climbed another three. I sent her back to shielding the commoners and keeping casualties at zero. I was burning to know how she'd suddenly hit that hard, but the middle of a fight wasn't the moment, so I waited back in and started mopping up the small fry myself.

I wasn't worried about Windsor since her monster was clearly almost finished. Once she'd seen I was safe, the anxiety drained out of her and she settled into a rhythm, bewildering the fish's swords off target and feeding it light arrows that whittleled its life down. And before long, it ran out of blood and died.

With the second big fish gone, the surrounding flying swordfish broke off and scattered into the deep, and I exhaled because a battle that long is exhausting in the head more than the body. Then Windsor came over and held out a skill stone, telling me this one had dropped, too. I almost laughed at my own luck. Another stone in one night.

But my hope deflated the moment I read it because it was a leap skill stone. Blue quality, 30% agility while leaping, restricted to the merman race in the archer class, useless to anyone aboard. I wondered idly if little blue could take it. Gave it a try and got my answer in a system line.

Skill stones can't be used on pets unless the pet has gained a hero class, which then lets them learn skills on their own. So be it. I did have a hero transfer card, but that one was spoken for, reserved for Huawa. So, I decided to run the leap stone through hundred transformations instead since I'd never transformed a skill stone before and was honestly curious what purple thing might fall out.

I told Windsor to go heal the wounded and make sure nobody died. And she nodded and got to work while I set Huhawa to gathering the spoils off the water. The flying swordfish kills being a serious haul. Once the healed commoners pitched in, the corpses came aboard fast and the battlefield was clean before long.

I sent everyone off to rest, then called Huawa over and gave her the two purple crystals, and she lit up, hugging my head and rubbing her tiny face against mine until I laughed and told her to eat already. She clutched both crystals, flapped her little wings, and flew off to the fishing pier, where she roosted at night.

After that, Windsor, Dark Moon, and I headed back to the core cabin. The Windsor walked oddly, keeping a careful distance from Dark Moon. During the fight, there had been no room to feel anything. But now that it was over, remembering the moment Dark Moon had caught us, she'd gone awkward and squirmy inside. I felt none of it.

In my own head, I had no intention of letting Dark Moon slip away since a cateared girl was exactly the sword I meant to win over, and we'd all be living openly together soon. So, what was there to be shy about? Back in the cabin, I sat on the sofa and ignored the two of them murmuring and blushing on the bed, checking my assets instead.

Nearly 4,000 white stone now, because dangerous as the ambush had been, it had paid well. I weighed my options. Hold the stone and dump it tomorrow on ice sails to rush a wooden boat upgrade or sink it into technology right now. I went with technology since the whole level wasn't urgent, whereas faster propulsion would carry me to my destination sooner.

I opened the technology interface and found multiore linkage power propulsion technology at level two. The upgrade asking 2,000 white stone and I paid without flinching. Then I bumped ship thermal energy technology to level two for another,000 so I could bank more thermal charge while the weather ran hot. I'd have pushed further, but my purse was empty, so the rest could wait for tomorrow, which dropped me back under a thousand white stone.

No matter, ice sails would refill it. I closed the panel, and my eyes drifted to Windsor, and dark moon stretched out on the bed.

The two of them lay face down, and I crossed from the sofa to the bed and gave Windsor a light tap, which drew a startled little cry and a flushed complaining look over her shoulder as she asked what I thought I was doing, embarrassed enough already in front of Dark Moon without me making it worse. Dark Moon's cheeks colored too.

Her thoughts clearly drifting back to what she'd overheard. Her body warming. I paid none of it any mind. Just hopped onto the bed and wedged myself in between them since my affinity with Dark Moon sat at 75 now and a bit of closeness wouldn't put her off. And a little contact tends to deepen a bond and nudge that affinity higher anyway.

I slid an arm around Windsor, my hand tracing her spine, and asked what the two of them had been whispering about, ignoring her embarrassment, figuring we'd best get used to this. Windsor went still and both girls dropped their heads since their talk had been girl secrets they'd never share with me. So I let it go before they got genuinely upset and changed tac.

I asked Dark Moon how she'd killed that flying swordfish so easily since by rights her current strength shouldn't have managed it. And Windsor, who'd wondered the same, looked over, too. Dark Moon didn't hide it, explaining it was the effect of her moonshadow skill.

When I asked since I couldn't read my hero skill details myself, she said it was a special one usable only under a moonlit night, time limited, and that while it ran, her strength simply doubled. That floored me because for all its limits, doubling raw strength is monstrous. And I pictured her at level 30 casting it and felt a chill.

She truly earned the Night Hunter name. Terrifying in the dark, well past Windsor on a moonlit field. I pressed on, asking about darkness erosion, and she said any strike that landed dropped the target into a weakened state, so a debuff. And from watching her, I'd already guessed darkness blade reshaped her weapon and sharpened it.

I told them to rest, and Windsor promptly ordered me to the sofa, bluntter than I'd braced for, which told me the night's excitement had genuinely rattled her. I sighed for effect and lamented that I was apparently still bound for the sofa. What a miserable life, and got up to go. Windsor looked faintly reluctant, but held her tongue since all of this was my doing.

Then, Dark Moon surprised everyone by suggesting the Lord take the bed. Catching the look on both our faces, she realized how that sounded and hurried to clarify that she meant the two of us should have the bed and she'd take the sofa. Windsor relaxed, having briefly feared Dark Moon was proposing something else.

While I felt a flicker of disappointment because, of course, there was no such luck, though it was worth a shot. So, I said Dark Moon was clearly the one who cared about me with a pointed glance at Windsor and announced that since Dark Moon insisted, I'd take the bed.

Windsor looked a little wounded since that made it sound like she didn't care when she'd been the one creeping over in the dead of night. As dark moon moved to rise, I caught her hand and told her the bed was huge and there was no need to exile herself. We could all rest on it together.

Both girls turned identical looks on me, the kind you give a wild animal, Windsor glaring outright, so I rushed to explain that I only meant Windsor should sleep in the middle and separate us since the bed easily fit four or five. I knew sleeping under one cover was off the table, but a little proximity could only help.

Reassured, Windsor stopped protesting and turned to gauge Dark Moon, then coaxed her, saying they should just do as I'd said since the sofa couldn't compare to the bed. Dark Moon hadn't wanted to agree, content enough with the sofa. But between Windsor's coaxing and my hopeful stare, something in her shifted, and she said with practice calm, that it was fine.

I told everyone to settle down before we wasted any more of the night, pulled Windsor over, and rolled to the innermost edge, while Dark Moon lay beside Windsor on the outside, her heart pounding so hard I half imagined I could hear it. Shy as she was, Windsor still curled into me and buried her face against my chest, and I kissed her forehead and wished her good night.

She caught me winking at her and understood at once that I'd been needling her on purpose all along, so she pinched the soft flesh at my waist and gave an indignant little huff. The cabin went quiet, leaving only the sound of three heartbeats in the dark. Dawn came. It was the fourth day of the week. I opened my eyes to find I'd somehow migrated to the middle of the bed.

Windsor and Dark Moon, each with a leg thrown across me, a happy sort of weight to wake under. Their lashes were trembling, so I knew they were already awake and only pretending. But I let them keep the pretense. I eased free, grabbed the cooler, and headed out because today was the day I made real money.

Once I'd gone, Windsor and Dark Moon let their eyes open and traded a look, color rising in both their faces. Windsor weathered it better, having long since grown comfortable with me. Her shyness today, owing mostly to its being the first time she'd shared a bed with me in front of an outsider.

Dark Moon was far more reserved since this had been her first such closeness with a man at all, beginning with a shared pillow, which left waves rolling through her quiet heart. Looking at Windsor now, she felt more than shy. She felt uneasy because Windsor was the Lord's woman, and she'd clung to me in the night, and she half expected reproach.

Windsor read the tangle in her eyes easily enough, took one of Dark Moon's hands, and said with a small smile not to worry, that if Dark Moon liked the Lord, she wouldn't object in the least. That only flustered Dark Moon more, and she protested quickly that she had no such thoughts, Sister Windsor, because she cared deeply about Windsor's opinion of her and dreaded opening a rift between them.

Windsor sat up, unbothered by what the motion revealed, patted Dark Moon's head, and said she needn't fret over any jealousy, that she could see plainly the Lord was interested in her. So, if Dark Moon liked him, she ought to be a little braver. Studying that earnest face, Dark Moon understood Windsor truly meant to comfort her, not test her.

And she shifted to lay her head on Windsor's lap, looking up to ask whether Sister Windsor really didn't mind about the Lord. Windsor rolled her eyes at the doubt and said, "Of course she minded. " And when Dark Moon, puzzled, asked then why she'd allow another woman to pursue him. Windsor answered as if it were obvious, because she could see the Lord liked Dark Moon, so naturally she had to help him.

Had I heard that, I'd have been genuinely moved since Windsor loved me so completely that whatever I wanted, she'd find a way to make it mine. The answer clearly blindsided Dark Moon, who hadn't imagined Windsor so wholly devoted to protecting me. Since fine as the Lord was, surely it shouldn't run that deep. And in her place, she'd have had a few opinions about the Lord chasing other women.

So she asked whether Windsor wasn't afraid that with others around, the Lord would grow distant from her. Windsor laughed it off, saying, "Of course not. How could I ever be distant from her? " Which showed how well she knew me. Then she patted Dark Moon's head once more, rubbing the little black cat ears, and told her again to remember if she liked him to be braver.

Dark Moon gave a soft hum, and Windsor told her to get up. The two of them rose and came out of the core cabin to find me sprawled on a lounge chair under the sun shade. A small table beside me set with iced fruit and chilled drinks, and they walked over and sat.

I turned to Windsor and told her to come fish while I went for the ice, handing her the resource fishing rod and passing Huawa from my arm into Dark Moons. Windsor took the rod and started casting and Dark Moon cradled Huawa, clearly taken with the dainty little thing. First, I drew out the hero transfer card from yesterday and used it on Huawa.

Then checked her panel. Huawa, fire elemental spirit, legendary, level six, her stats in the low 40s, 430 mana, now reading profession hero, fire element controller with her old skills intact, a white small staff equipped, loyalty 100, affinity 100. Her information had transformed completely, and I hadn't expected the card to do so much. Her panel looked exactly like Windsor's now.

Nothing about it suggesting a pet at all. Beyond the hero profession, she'd gained fire element controller, which I figured was the class effect that came bundled with a fire elemental spirit. The last line surprised me, too, since I hadn't realized her loyalty and affinity toward me both sat at full value.

And it dawned on me that those two hidden stats run maxed for every summoned pet and only surface now that she'd become a hero. Down the line, if I landed another hero transfer card, I'd change Little Blue's class because the hero professions perks were numerous and the growth potential far outstripped the non-heroes.

I closed the panel, set the thinking aside, and started making ice, having the commoners cart each batch off to the storage room as it came. Time slid by, and my came before me, bowed, and announced breakfast was ready. I got up and called Windsor and Dark Moon, and Huawa flew out of Dark Moon's arms to land against me, where I caught her quickly.

Looking into her round eyes, I had the sense that becoming a hero had left her sharper and more alert. Back in the core cabin, I sat at the table Mory had already laid, glancing over a light morning spread of porridge, steamed buns, and a quick stirfry of cabbage with vinegar. I ate while reviewing the three items Windsor had hauled in that morning.

The first was a pleasant shock. A mineral excavator blueprint, blue quality, granting the ability to mine ore, and add brick and tile to my territo's construction, demanding 200 iron block, 100 copper block, 40 rubber, and 100 electronic components. A blue blueprint meant a purple item if I ran it through 100 transformations. So, I pocketed it happily and turned to the second.

The second was shaped like a compass. Spirit eye named it a treasure detector. Green quality, able to sense treasures within a thousand meter radius and guide me right to them. Usable for 3 hours. Not bad at all. Even capped at 3 hours, and I was quietly pleased since a permanent version would surely have rated well above green.

Those three hours weren't to be squandered on a whim. I'd hold the detector until exactly the right opening and ring every drop of value out of it. Then I looked at the third item.

The third reward turned out to be a small cloth bag, and when I ran my spirit eye over it, the appraisal named it a mist tree seed, a green item that could be planted around a territory to release a concealing haze. The description promised it would hide my territo's information and keep prying eyes from probing how strong or weak I actually was.

That was not bad at all because a screen of mist meant any enemy who came sniffing around would have to guess at my real strength instead of reading it like a price tag. All three poles had been solid which made me wonder, only half joking, whether I ought to retire from fishing entirely and hand the rod to Windsor for good.

Her luck ran circles around mine, and a merchant who refuses to delegate a more profitable worker is just throwing money into the sea. I tuck the seed away with the rest. Eight and decided to dive before anything else so Windsor and the crew could hunt while I worked. No sense letting either job stall the other.

I triggered the dive, let the wooden boat sink, and handed the controls to my navigator so I could get back to the only labor that mattered right now. Turning sea water into cold cash. When we surfaced near the shore, I did not bother sorting the catch since night was the time for inventory and daylight was for selling.

I opened the trading channel and started posting ice and steady lots. 10 cubes for 90 white stone, 100 for 900, one batch after another until the listing stacked up. Then I shouted across the world channel that I had put ice on the market, that the stock was thin, and that it was first come, first served. I also nudged Wang Moth to spread the word and slipped him 50 cubes for his trouble.

Though honestly, his promotion was a courtesy more than a necessity. The moment my announcement landed, the whole world channel lit up like a struck match. Someone marveled that the great Hashin had actually dained to show his face, which apparently happened about as often as a snowstorm in this heat.

Another demanded to know why the number one lord was pedalling ice cubes when everybody already had a personal store and asked whether the sun had cooked my brain. A faster hand crowed that he had snatched 50 pieces the instant the listings appeared.

A slower one wailed that not a single cube was left and got told with great sympathy that his hand speed was no good and he should break up with his partner to practice. The real point came through under the noise that my cubes were the same size as the store ones but priced one white stone cheaper each.

So a man who bought 50 saved 50 whit stone outright. Several said 50 cubes felt like enough to get them through this whole hot stretch. And then the channel dissolved into a chorus, begging me for more. I had not expected my whole stockpile to evaporate that quickly. So, I went straight back to grinding out fresh cubes, draining my mana, listing the batch, and waiting for it to refill.

A message blinked up on my friends list, and the sender was Le Kio asking whether I still had ice and wanting 100 pieces, no greeting, no warm-up. I told her I had nothing on hand and pointed out that a player like her hardly lacked the loose change to buy ice, so why come find me specifically?

She replied that saving a little was still saving and that when I had more, she wanted first refusal. I turned her down flat and told her to go fight for cubes in the trading channel like everyone else, then closed the window before she could argue. On her end, she was probably clenching those pink little fists and fuming, which in a small petty way I enjoyed picturing.

There were actually three reasons I refused her a private batch. First, she genuinely did not need my discounted ice to survive, so withholding it cost her nothing real. Second, dumping cubes into the world channel saved as many ordinary blue star players as possible, and a few cheaper whit stone might be exactly what let some of them scrape through the disaster, keeping the spirit domain side stronger overall.

Third was pure publicity, since I planned to have Wang Mock broadcast that I had turned down a top 10 beauty's private request in favor of the common crowd, and a reputation like that occasionally pays dividends nobody can predict. That way I delayed neither my profits nor my good deeds, which is the kind of arithmetic a merchant lives for.

For the rest of the daylight, I just repeated the loop. Make ice, sell ice, refill, on into the night. Somewhere in the middle, Lie Chun came by. And since he had sold me good things before and was not exactly swimming in white stone, I let him have 50 cubes. When night fell and dinner was done, I used the gap while my mana recovered to tally my assets.

And the figure that came back was over 17,700 white stone, which honestly shocked me. Some of that was carryover, plus the day's hunting and fishing. But even after stripping those out, the ice cubes alone had cleared more than 10,000. That was upgrade money. So, I went straight for the wooden boat, which wanted 2,000 wood, 4,000 iron nails, 200 copper, and a single ship core.

I spent 10,000 whit stone buying the materials outright and pushed the upgrade through. A wash of light swept over the whole vessel, and it grew longer and wider. But this time, it also grew taller, and a new staircase opened near the fishing corridor leading down into the hole. The upgrade had stopped just bolting more onto one deck and had started adding entire floors instead.

When I checked the readout, my craft had been renamed a tower ship, level one again, 45 m long, and 15 wide. The first floor now held a kitchen, a large living room, a public toilet, an observation deck, the fishing corridor, the mast, and the rudder. While the second floor below carried the core cabin, two more large living rooms, four small ones, a warehouse, and another toilet.

Its power still ran on multi-or linkage propulsion and its technologies listed ship drainage, thermal energy, automation, multi-ore linkage propulsion, and diving at a weakened level two with diving as its special ability. The level snapping back to one confirmed what I had suspected that ships reset their stage every six levels exactly the way talent ranks did.

I climbed up to the new observation deck first, shaped like an arrow tower and perfect for watching the distance, then descended to find my core cabin had swelled into two rooms, a bathroom, and a kitchen. Mory could move into the new small bedroom and become my personal chef while a second cook handled meals for the rest of the commoners.

As for where that left dark moon, she would keep sleeping in my room since that arrangement had already happened once. Before I called the tour finished, I pulled up the next level's requirement to see whether a ship core would be demanded again because that detail mattered a great deal. The next level wanted 4,000 wood, 8,000 iron nails, 400 copper blocks, and one ship core.

And seeing that confirmed, I let out a long breath. It meant every level from here would cost a ship core. And that was the optimistic read since nobody knew whether the third stage might demand several per level. If each upgrade passed the next stage swallowed multiple cores, building any truly powerful warship would carry a brutal price tag.

A navy really is an army that exists to set money on fire. With the upgrade done, my mana had nearly refilled. So, I started making ice again. Having decided to skip rest entirely tonight, I sent Windsor and Dark Moon off to sleep since they could not help right now and were better off conserving energy for whatever tomorrow's hunt might throw at us.

The deck lay quiet and pale, faint moonlight scattering across the water until the whole sky seemed to shimmer with silver while Lee Ruk steered and the sailors Wang Wei and Wang Rim kept the ship sliding forward.

This was my first night spent out on the open sea, and I stretched on a lounge chair, ate fruit, and sipped a bottle of chilled red wine, finding the whole thing had a flavor all its own. Once my mana ran dry again, I turned to sorting what the crew had fished and what Windsor's group had hunted. And since there were only four items, I appraised them one by one.

The first was a talent orb, green, granting a random talent on use. And I was thrilled that the very first pull was already excellent. Then I hesitated, weighing whether to use it raw for a guaranteed green talent or to gamble it through 100 transformations for something better. I did not much care about blue items, and the trade would only feel worth it if my talent spat out purple or gold.

In the end, I decided a guaranteed talent be a to die roll. So, I used the orb directly. The system informed me my talent slots were full and the orb could not be used, which made me curse out loud at the realization that all my careful weighing had been pointless.

Once I calmed down, I noticed the prompt said, "Currently unable, not permanently unable," which quietly told me these talent slots could be increased somehow. So, I asked Chan Mo directly how a lord went about gaining talent slots, mostly to test whether she would even answer.

She told me that reaching the third stage, general rank, granted one slot, and reaching the fifth stage, emperor rank, granted another, and that further slots could be earned through certain special rewards. I sat there genuinely stunned that Shano had bothered to answer me at all, and then I started picking the reply apart for everything it implied.

The plainest meaning was that I would have to wait until at least level 13 for a second slot and level 25 for a third, while the special reward path was clearly hard enough that I set it aside without much hope.

Another thread in her answer caught me more than the slots did, which was that each stage seemed to carry a formal title since she had named the third stage general rank, and the fifth emperor rank. So, I asked why my information panel did not show my current rank and how the ranks were divided in the first place.

Shano laid out all five for me: soldier rank, knight rank, general rank, king rank, and emperor rank, and added that the full information display would only update once a novice lord established a territory and became a true lord.

That explained a great deal since it meant I was effectively an intern lord right now with half my permissions locked and only after planting a real territory would I get regularized and have the rest open to me. Interns get no rights anywhere apparently, not even in a survival game run by a goddess.

Since the talent orb was useless to me for now, I sheld it to feed through 100 transformations later and moved on to the next item. The second was an ice crystal tower blueprint. Green, a defensive structure that fired freezing rays to damage and slow enemies, needing wood, stone, and five ice gems.

The tower sounded useful, but ice gem rang far more advanced than plain wood, and I had no idea where to find it, so I filed the blueprint away to worry about after I had a territory. The third was a medical room blueprint, white, a life- related building that keeps residents healthy and strengthens their sense of belonging, requiring only wood and stone, which would round out my basic infrastructure nicely.

The fourth was a fish micro resource card, white, usable in a territo's rivers and lakes to produce some fish and add a food source. That last one made me curious whether other resource cards existed for iron ore, fruit forests, gems, and the like. And the possibility had me looking forward to the future.

Overall, it was a decent hall, white and green, but every item patched some gap I would feel keenly in the early days of a settlement. So, I stored them and went back to ice while my mana refilled. About then, Huawa flew over from the fishing deck and landed in my arms, but her head against my chest over and over, and I smiled and rubbed her hair.

Every time she came to me, she carried this odd settling calm, as if my heart slowed just from holding her. And traveling with such a cheerful companion made me feel genuinely lucky. The thing was, she had enormous growth potential and would one day be a real pillar of my territory, which was exactly what made her grow such a stubborn problem.

She could not level by killing sea monsters and could only grow by eating spirit crystals, purple crystals, and similar materials, which throttled her advancement badly. Sometime soon, I would have to learn precisely what an elemental spirit like her could consume, then buy it in bulk and spend heavily to cultivate her.

I was still chewing on that when a friend request pinged in the dead middle of the night, and I wondered who on earth wanted me at this hour. When I opened the channel, the sender was Lee Kio, which left me a little baffled about what she could possibly want. Now.
